The 12 Best Riflescopes We Tested this Year: All Our Optics Test Winners

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Published Nov 6, 2026 2:53 PM EST

We may earn revenue from the products available on this page and participate in affiliate programs. Learn More ›

Get the hottest outdoor news from Outdoor Life.

Optics are one the most intensive and expansive categories we tackle. It’s a category we’ve been testing for as long as

This year we conducted two in-depth rifle scope tests. We kicked off the year in south Texas at the Thermal Ranch where we tested the latest in thermal scope technology while hunting hogs, predators, and shooting on the range. Then we met in eastern Montana to evaluate hunting scopes, LPVOs, and precision scopes. We tested them for their lowlight capability, tracking, ergonomics, overall shoot-ability, and we even drop tested them.

I encourage you to read our various in-depth scope buying guides: Best Hunting Scopes, Best Thermal Scopes, Best LPVOs, and Best Scopes for Deer Hunting. But, if you want a summary of just the award winners, this guide is for you. Below you’ll find the top performers from this year’s optics tests.

With this big, bright, and surprisingly sophisticated R7, Bushnell is restoring its relevance as a first-order hunting optic brand. The first focal-plane scope has an open tree-style reticle that’s anchored by an illuminated segmented circle and floating center dot that enables precision work as well as fast holdover and wind-informed shots at game.

For hunters and target shooters who would rather dial, the pull-to-turn elevation turret is nicely indexed and moves with positivity. The windage dial is capped.

Bushnell has invested heavily in the R7 line, which is the best of the Good/Better/Best product-line hierarchy that started with the R3 a couple years ago and continued with the R5 last year. The company has four different configurations of the R7, released just this month: a 1-8×24 LPVO, a 2-10×42, and a 3-15×50 in addition to the 4-20×50. The lower magnification scopes are available in second-plane reticles, but the two higher-mag versions come in either first- or second-plane reticles and either MIL or MOA, giving buyers a crazy number of variations on the platform.

We tested the second-plane MOA version of the 4-20×50, but for its versatility, performance on a wide range of targets and distances, and its flawless handling, we named the FFP R7 not only best Bushnell in the test, but the best overall hunting scope in the field of 25 excellent competitors. They include Nightforce’s new NX6, Leupold’s VX-4HD, and Zeiss’s new V3 families, along with some excellent submissions from Vortex, Trijicon, and Burris.

For its overall performance, the Bushnell R7 4-20×50 is this year’s Editor’s Choice winner as the top hunting scope of 2026.

The scope stacked points with a combination of good ED Prime glass, clear indexing on its controls, tight and positive turret and focus controls, and an illuminated Hybrid Dot Tree reticle that splits the difference between a precision competition and a hunting reticle. Its zero stop, which we’re glad to see on this type scope, is a 6-screw affair that’s a hassle to set, but works wonderfully as a solid backstop on elevation turret travel.

We worried about the durability of the R7, but it passed our inaugural drop test without skipping a beat. We dropped the scope three times from three feet on concrete covered by a 2-inch pad, and the Bushnell retained its zero.

Of course, our test teased out some deficiencies. The reticle references aren’t especially visible until about 7x, and I’d like to see one or two more intensity steps to the center-dot and segmented circle illumination to make them pop in daylight.

But if you’re looking for a rimfire or long-range competition scope that you can easily transition into a big-game hunt, this is a wonderful choice. The price, at about $950, is fair for the versatility and fine handling of this true crossover scope.

Read Next: The Best Riflescopes for Hunting

The problem with many budget rifle scopes is that they distract from disappointing glass with whiz-bang features like tactical turrets and add-ons like cheap rings, power-changing levers, and illuminated reticles.

​The Burris Fullfield 3-12×42 has none of those. Instead, it’s a throwback, built on a 1-inch tube with capped turrets and a second-plane duplex reticle. This is the scope that appeals to your buddy who wants to hand-crank their pickup windows. By keeping things simple, Burris has produced a new classic that might be the perfect deer-hunting scope in our test of nearly 25 hunting scopes. That it’s able to sell the Fullfield 3-12×42 for under $300 made it the unanimous pick for the Great Buy award in this year’s rifle scope test.

​The Burris had plenty of competition for the honor. Vortex’s 3-9×40 Crossfire HD has many of the same features, second-plane reticle, and fuss-free performance for a little less money. But we felt the build and tight handling of the Burris make it a longer-wearing optic, and we tested that hunch by subjecting the scope to our inaugural drop test, which it passed with flying colors.

​Don’t expect the Fullfield to engage steel targets way out there. Instead, this is a straight-ahead hunting scope. Think straight-wall carbine, slug gun, rimfires, or just about any deer rifle on the market.

​“It’s a set-it-and-forget-it scope,” notes tester Nate Ward. “Not a lot of moving parts to break or batteries to fail.”

​Indeed, the Burris doesn’t have an illuminated reticle, and its parallax correction is set at 100 yards. The standard plex reticle is intended to be fast but not precise, since there are no reticle holds in the image plane. Hunters who want more reticle references should consider Burris’s excellent Ballistic E3 reticle. If you’d rather have illumination, the Wind Ret reticle is available for another $100.

​The standard-issue Fullfield has some nice features, including distinctive red accents on the power-changing ring and turret dials and rezeroable turrets. But we also noted some misses. It has a distracting greenish coating that’s especially noticeable in low-light conditions. Its turret indexing is so tiny that it’s hard to confirm when you’re zeroed. And some testers struggled to get both the target and reticle sharp in the same focus plane beyond 500 yards.

But for a good image, simple and repeatable controls, and abundant versatility, the Burris Fullfield 3-12×42 checks nearly every box for a hunting scope. By doing it for under $300, it is the very definition of a Great Buy.

Nightforce is known for building battle-ready riflescopes for long-range precision shooting. But their new NX6 product line is designed specifically for hunters. I used the 3-18X50 F1 version with a MIL reticle on an elk hunt this fall and found it to be an excellent open-country hunting scope. It had strong image quality and low-light performance. It’s a hefty scope, but not absurdly so, like some other precision optics.

One of the key features of the NX6 line is the new FieldSet turret system. Once you’ve established your zero, any simple tool — a coin, knife, or flat rock — will loosen the exposed turret cap, allowing you to reset your zero and set the zero stop. The stop can be set a full revolution above zero, or 2 MILs.

One of the many cool things about this system is that any NX6 model except the 1-6x can be converted from capped to exposed turret, and the standard exposed turret can be traded for a custom BDC turret. It’s another indication that the NX6 is positioned to be the ultimate versatile scope family.

The scope comes with a versatile illumination system that lights up the entire reticle. This is a relatively advanced and expensive scope, but for spun-up riflemen who are looking to shoot deer at longer ranges, it’s an excellent option. — Alex Robinson

This short- to medium-range workhorse from Nightforce was years in development, but it was worth both the wait and the weight. While it’s a compact unit, measuring under 11 inches long and weighing just over a pound, the NX6 feels much more substantial.

That weight and durability will make this a battalion optic for uniformed services around the world. But it will also make it a very good pick for a 3-Gun competitor or a fly-in Alaskan bear hunter. Nightforce has had the entire NX6 line in incubation for years, as an answer to scopes like Leupold’s Mark 4HD and Mark 5HD, Vortex’s Razor HD and the Vudu from EOTECH. It was worth the wait.

We tested many configurations of the NX6 line in our other rifle scope tests, but the standout of the new family is this smart, sharp, and extremely versatile 1-6×24 LPVO. For bringing best-in-class controls, a smart first focal-plane reticle, useful illumination, and a built-to-last package, the Nightforce NX6 1-6×24 wins our Editor’s Choice award for the top LPVO in this year’s test.

The Nightforce comes in three reticle types, either the standard illuminated 4A duplex, the hash-style FC-6c BDC reticle that’s configured for multi-gun competition, or the illuminated FC-DMx in the first focal-plane, which is the version we tested.

The reticle features an illuminated segmented circle with a floating center dot that, on increasing the magnification, reveals a 1 MRAD aiming grid. At lower mags, the clustered circle-dot arrangement makes a coherent and fast red-dot sight. At higher mags, that grid comes into view and assists precision bullet placement out to as far a rifle with this type scope can shoot.

The old-fashioned locking eyepiece diopter is strong and reliable, but it’s also stiff and slow, especially if you’re trading the scope between shooters with different optical prescriptions. The eyebox can be stingy at higher magnifications. We’d like to see a couple of brighter steps in the illumination, and we think the sweet configuration for this scope is 1.5-9x so that the reticle’s precision references might have even more visibility.

But the Nightforce behaves as you’d expect it to, and its 6-times magnification range seems optimized for this LPVO platform. The controls are tight and smooth. The 30mm main tube is rigid and stiff. The entire unit feels like you could use it as a falling wedge in the big woods around Nightforce’s northern Idaho home. In our experience, it would probably excel at that task, too, and then stack bullets in just about any competition or hunting scenario you can imagine.

At $1,800, the 1-6×24 is pricey. But that price doesn’t feel out of line for an heirloom optic that will trade across not only rifle platforms, but generations.

Sig Sauer’s best-selling LPVO just got smaller, tighter, and brighter. The TANGO-MSR Compact brings all the versatility of the original in a package that measures a puny 8.4 inches and comes pre-mounted on a very good cantilever unimount for the screaming price of $399.

For packing in so much value in a tidy, powerful package, the TANGO-MSR Compact is our Great Buy as the best deal of this year’s Outdoor Life LPVO test.

We’ll detail the two things that gave us pause before gushing about all the features of this scope that make it a winner. The glass isn’t that good. We detected some pincushion aberrations that are noticeable when panning at lower magnifications, and the overall resolution is average. The eyebox is pretty stingy, meaning that when you throw the scope up to engage a target, it’s easy to be out of alignment with the image.

But everything else about this little marvel is spot-on. Generally, we like to see first focal-plane reticles in LPVOs because they boost the platform’s versatility. At their lower magnifications, FFP reticles act as a red-dot sight, and at higher magnifications, they perform as precision optics. But the second-plane arrangement in the TANGO-MSR — the MOA-based reticle is called the BDC6 — is nearly as useful for those purposes. The inverted-horseshoe center aiming arrangement blazes bright in daylight conditions, and the BDC grid is useful for placing bullets with precision on targets out to 500 yards. The only real tradeoff is that the scope must be set at its highest magnification for the bullet-drop subtensions to be accurate.

We put the scope through a battery of close-range fast-fire drills, and atop an AR carbine it’s a nimble and effective personal-defense scope. Then we slowed things down and rang steel out to a quarter-mile with the scope, using both its BDC references but just as importantly, its fine MOA-based windage dots to hold into the prairie breeze.

The TANGO-MSR is optimized for this AR-15 work, but it would be great on a straight-wall cartridge carbine, muzzleloader, or a pistol-caliber carbine, and it’s made for 3-Gun competitions. The super-compact size makes it a great fit for rimfires. And the price makes it accessible for just about any shooter. The addition of its mount means that it’s ready to rock, right out of the box.

Pulsar’s Thermion scope has been on the leading edge of thermal tech and performance since it introduced the XG50 in 2020. The platform has scaled up with each new iteration, and this year’s rangefinding XL60 hits new highs in sensitivity, run time, durability, and ease of operation.

The fact that a 1024-pixel thermal led a fairly small but impressive field of 1280-pixel scopes says a lot about the Pulsar’s ability to enhance image quality with a best-in-class display and processing that boosts contrast of its relatively underpowered sensor.

The Thermion2’s most noticeable upgrade is its big 60mm objective lens, but its 10-hour run time is impressive, and its 2,800-yard detection range is among the best in our test. As close readers of our test know, we quibble with the published detection ranges of thermals; we think “recognition range” is a much more useful metric. In the case of the Pulsar, we put the recognition range at about 550 yards, plenty for nearly any hunting situation we’d expect to encounter at night and among the best in our field.

The Thermion2 is built on a 30mm tube, and standard medium-height rings make mounting a cinch. Also easy: the scope’s intuitive 3-button navigation. One button zooms the image, another records or stops recording content, and the third navigates the menu, which is accessed via a rubberized button on the scope’s left turret. Zeroing is a cinch and field deployment is among the fastest and least confounding in the field.

“This scope fired up fast and was quick to deploy on the tripod,” notes tester Derrick Ratliff, who also praised the spring-activated lens cap that eagerly swings out of the way. The ambidextrous focus control, two finger-grabbing dials on either side of the rangefinder tower, is easy to operate, even with gloved fingers.

The round display is especially easy on the eyes, resembling the dimensions of a daylight scope, and the high-resolution display supports the sharpest picture-in-picture we’ve ever tested. Other plusses: the 10-hour run time and easy interchange with Pulsar’s excellent StreamVision2 mobile app. The rangefinder is fast and precise.

We handed down some demerits, as well. The frequent refresh is so abrupt and jarring that it interferes with operation. “I’m surprised the refresh is as harsh as it is given the price,” notes Ratliff. And we wish Pulsar would abandon its proprietary rechargeable batteries and join the industry movement toward interchangeable 18650 cells. The Pulsar cells are powerful, but if you lose one you’re on the hook for a pricey replacement.

But overall, the Thermion2 LRF XL60 is a first-order thermal scope. It’s fast, clear, easy to use, and was the consensus choice for the best weapon sight of this year’s thermal test.

If you’re looking for a do-it-all thermal, it’s hard to beat this versatile workhorse from DNT. The Hydra is probably at its best as a weapon-mounted scope, a role that is enhanced with its decent laser rangefinder and basic but capable ballistic calculator. But snap the DNT off its quick-detach rail mount, and the unit becomes a nimble thermal monocular.

​We tested this capability, zeroing the Hydra to our 22 Creedmoor, but then liberating it from the gun to scan the Texas brush country for pigs and coyotes. Once we had acquired a target, we clipped the device back on the rifle and made the kill. In the daylight, we measured this talent, timing the span between scan-and-fire and then assessing its ability to hold its zero. We could make the transition in under 10 seconds, with no appreciable stray in its aiming point.

​DNT claims the Hydra can also function as a clip-on thermal. We didn’t test this capability, but given its eye-filling display, low mounting profile that would easily mate with a low-power variable daylight scope, and generally responsive controls, we can’t quibble with that additional functionality.

​We liked the double-tough metallic housing and oversized eyepiece of the Hydra, and had no trouble zeroing or running the unit in the field. Its 1.5x base magnification is a little low, but it provides a satisfyingly wide field of view as both a monocular and a scope.

​The 4-hour run time was a little disappointing. We had to make sure to have charged 18650 cells ready to drop into the Hydra in the course of a long sit. And its display, optimized for pulling double duty as a viewer and a scope, appears to be far away down a long tube. The display has two features that are more distraction than aiming aids: a cant indicator that shows when the scope is off its vertical axis, and an angle measure that shows positive and negative incline. The image would be cleaner without those two adornments that don’t add much to its performance.

​But overall, testers considered the image to be crisper than its specs indicated, and the tactile LRF button is easy to find and operate in any of the Hydra’s three modes.

​“It’s a good option for a first thermal purchase,” notes tester Scott Einsmann. “It’s all you need for 100 yards and in. The mount needs work, though. It’s not particularly well thought-out.”

​Note that DNT makes 10 versions of the Hydra, four with laser rangefinders and six without, so be prepared to wade through a lot of options in the product line. But the HS635R is one of the rare bargains in the thermal category. For about $2,500 you get a serviceable viewer and a very good weapon-mounted scope with a solid build, an honest warranty, and plenty of performance. For all those reasons, the DNT Hydra HS635R is our Great Buy for this year’s Outdoor Life thermal test.

DNT Hydra HS635R Specs: Sensor Resolution 640×512

With this 12.5-inch gem, Vortex has redefined the form factor of a high-performing precision scope. While other brands have tried to package a first-order reticle and repeatable controls in a compact build, they haven’t been able to pull off the task as well as Vortex has, thanks to the index-matched ED glass from Japan.

The versatility of the latest iteration of the Razor HD elevated the Vortex just a smidge above its two other competitors for the best precision scope of the year, the new Nightforce NX6 5-30×56 and the surprising Sig BRAVO 6T-BDX 5-30×56 “digitally enhanced” scope.

Beyond the quality of its glass, which makes the Razor HD Gen III useful at both the lowest and highest end of its versatile magnification range, the Vortex’s controls deserve special mention. While the Outdoor Life test team found them a little tight out of the box, the turrets move with surgical precision and the pull-to-adjust illumination module is a fine piece of machining. The compact build doesn’t detract from dialing to your heart’s content. The MOA version of the scope that we tested has nearly 160 MOA (46 MILS in the MRAD version) of elevation adjustment.

The scope has a smart revolution indicator on the elevation turret. After you rotate a full revolution, a silver plunger protrudes from the dial. A second rotation futher extends the plunger, which has an O-ring to ensure moisture doesn’t work into the erector system. The system allows users to know where they are in the dial without extending the height of the low-profile elevation turret.

The EBR-70 reticle won’t particularly wow precision shooters who have come to expect its combination of speed and granularity. It’s fine even though we occasionally had to slow down to manually count windage MOA dots for some extreme hold-off shots.

For its ability to mount low on a wide variety of platforms, its dead-on precision, and its excellent glass and controls, the Vortex Razor HD Gen III is the Editor’s Choice winner as the top-scoring precision scope of the year. With a real-world street price of around $2,800, it’s a significant investment but for an all-around tactical, competition, and even hunting scope, it packs a ton of performance in its 12.5-inch build.

Outdoor Life’s Shooting Editor, John B. Snow, is a competitive shooter and expert in all things long range. He wrote an article and recorded a video on his top precision scope recommendations. If he ad to choose just one though, the Nightforce ATACR 7-35×56 is his top pick. Here’s what he has to say about this scope. Click here to read his full guide.

The Nightforce ATACR 7-35×56 FFP for a long time has set standard for this category. It’s durability under the worst battlefield conditions is the stuff of legend. No type of sporting optic is as complex (or subject to failure) as a rifle scope, and Nightforce built its reputation with products that could stand up to serious abuse. Beyond that, the controls on these scopes are superlative – the ATACR hits the ball out of the park with its ergonomics.

And the Mil-XT reticle is among the best out there. The zero stop is complex by today’s standards, but not difficult to set once you know how. In the not-too-distant future I suspect Nightforce will update the ATACR platform given its age, but it is still one of the most capable pieces of glass out there and one I turn to again and again when it counts, as when shooting ELR matches.

Like the new Nightforce NX6, we could write a tome on Zeiss’s new V3 family of second-plane scopes that are going to compete well with Leupold and Vortex for America’s favorite deer-hunting optic. We tested three versions with similar enough features that we’ll cover our general impressions before teasing apart some of the points of differentiation between the 3.5-10×42, 4.5-14×44, and 6-18×50.

​Note that this family is so new that we tested what Zeiss calls “pre-production” models of the V3. Some of our critiques, including indistinct turret clicks, have been improved in the mass-produced versions of the scopes.

The big news is that these premium Zeiss scopes are no longer made in Europe. They’re sourced in Japan, but if that suggests a decline in optical performance or brand integrity, relax. These represent the best of Japanese precision manufacturing, built around premium components and curated with American hunters in mind. That’s an important inflection; previous Zeiss rifle scopes were almost maddeningly European, with metric controls that often turned the wrong way.

The V3 line includes six models, from the rimfire-friendly 2.5-8×35 that will cost an appealing $300 to the all-around 3.5-10×42 up to the 6-18×50 that’s purpose-built for long shots in open country.

All three of our test samples featured Zeiss’s new exposed elevation turret with Ballistic Stop. The 3.5-10×42 has a dime-bright illuminated center aiming point but parallax fixed at 150 yards. The 4.5-14×44 has both independent parallax correction and illumination. Both these scopes have Zeiss’s Smart Hunting Reticle (SHR), which we found a bit too crowded. The 6-18×50 V3 has a non-illuminated XRR (eXtended Range) reticle, which we like better for fast target acquisition in dynamic hunting situations.

Like Vortex’s Crossfire HD family, the NX6 from Nightforce, and Leupold’s new VX-4HD family, the V3 has a number of variations on its basic theme of second-plane, 3-times zoom with prices that range from that $300 plinker to $950. Given the abundant attributes packed into the most feature-rich V3s, that price is right in line with its competitors.

The V3 has noticeably bright glass. Zeiss claims at least 90 percent light transmission through the entire optic, not just one or two lens elements. That brightness is achieved partly through wire-frame reticles that don’t require the additional lens elements of engraved reticles, but also with excellent glass and Zeiss’s proprietary coatings, which tame flaring, boost contrast, and maximize light transmission.

The test team appreciated the tactility and ease of adjusting the exposed elevation turret, but its operation held the entire line back from a higher score. The turret, with an impressive five revolutions of travel, wobbles slightly, and simply moves too easily. It’s important to note that turret performance is a function of the pre-production build. Following our test, Zeiss sent production models that have deeper detents and much more positive action in what could be called the “slow-speed” turret. The V3 dials allow only 10 MOA of travel per revolution, but because they have just 40 clicks, each gear is deeper and more positive than the shallower detents in “high-speed” turrets with 20 to 25 MOA of travel per revolution.

Because it doesn’t lock, some testers fretted that the exposed turret could get knocked off zero in a hard hunt. You can easily return the turret to its zero, thanks to the solid, mechanical ballistic stop, but we’d like to see a pull-to-turn locking feature on the exposed turret. Of course, you can always get the version with a capped elevation turret.

Our only other gripe was the crowded appearance of the SHR reticle. With its 1 MOA hashes clustered on both the horizontal and vertical stadia and no numeric references, we found ourselves counting aloud the holdover and holdoff references. We like the XRR reticle in the 6-18×50 better, largely because it’s more open, allowing for quicker, more assured holds.

​There’s plenty that we did like about this new V3 family. The center-dot illumination is sharp and bright, and enhances reticle visibility in the SHR configuration. The glass is excellent, with edge clarity and nice contrast, color fidelity, and that quantifiable brightness. We like the light weight of all the models. The 3.5-10×42 weighs less than a pound, even with the overlarge exposed elevation turret. We like the controls, which turn with style and confidence. Lastly, we really like the huge internal adjustment range of the V3. Some models feature twice the reticle adjustment of their peers.

​The entire V3 line is priced in the sweet spot considering its excellent glass, abundant features, quality of components, and impressive versatility of these light and very bright new scopes.

This might be the ultimate low-maintenance mountain scope. Zero this scope, cap the turrets, and go hunting. For those hunters and shooters distracted by the crazy amount of adjustability in a modern scope — exposed eager-to-turn turrets, busy reticles that require a math degree to comprehend, and illumination and parallax all requiring your attention before a shot — this stripped-down VX-4HD is your huckleberry.

​At 14.6 ounces, it is one of the best lightweight mountain-rifle scopes in this year’s test. “Belongs on my sheep rifle,” declared one tester.

​The team was sharply divided on the merits of this old-school optic. Half felt its simplicity was a feature. The other half thought its unilluminated “Hunt-Plex” reticle, 150-yard fixed parallax, and capped turrets are a liability in an era of scope adjustability.

​For the former, the 3-12×40’s excellent glass and “huntability” is the point. The fixed parallax didn’t affect focus; we were able to keep the reticle and target in sharp focus out beyond 500 yards, which is near the maximum range of this reticle. The eyebox is forgiving, meaning you don’t have to have a perfect eye alignment to see the entire image. Images were bright and vibrant in daylight, though the scope finished last in our low-light test, probably limited by the 40mm objective lens. The lightweight and low-profile mounting dimensions add to the Leupold’s versatility.

​But for the critics, versatility is limited by the unforgivable tactility of the turrets and the pedestrian aiming system.

​“The turrets are maddeningly hard to turn, too tight, and they have indistinct clicks,” noted one tester. “I can’t really hear or feel the clicks, and the turret indexing is very poor. This is a good set-and-forget hunting scope, but the turrets are a letdown, especially at the price” of about $800.

​The 3-12×40 version is the only VX-4HD with capped turrets. The rest of the family has the updated CDS-ZL2 elevation turrets. If Leupold revisits this model, it would further endear itself to those go-light hunters who want the combination of simplicity and a small measure of adjustability.

Like Nightforce, Zeiss, and Leupold, Vortex is completing the buildout of an entire rifle scope family. The Crossfire HD line, slowly replacing the Crossfire 2 line, has a scope for every hunter, from a handy LPVO to this high-mag optic that’s optimized for hunters but which has some stoke for target shooters.

​We tested other configurations of the family last year. This year we evaluated the 3-9×40 and 2-7×32 versions of the Crossfire HD line, so while this review is mainly focused on the 6-18×50 build, our perspectives apply to those other scopes with similar reticles and operating systems.

The 6-18×50 gained points for its hunter-centric BDC reticle, which has MOA-based hashes at 1.5, 4.5, and 7.5 MOA below the illuminated center aiming point. Those spacings conform to the drop of standard-velocity bullets zeroed at 100 yards or high-velocity and magnum calibers zeroed at 200 yards (Vortex ships a chart of zeroing recommendations with its scopes).

Users are encouraged to match their load ballistics with Vortex’s Long Range Ballistic Calculator to understand subtensions at various magnifications. That’s an important consideration, since published subtensions are only valid at the highest magnification of the second-plane reticle. For most hunters, who will throw away the reticle reference manual the minute they mount the scope, the 4-12×44 or 3-9×40 are more useful magnification ranges for the Dead-Hold BDC reticle than the 6-18×50 version. But when we plugged our specific ballistics into the Vortex calculator, we got magnification-adjusted holds out to 500 yards with center-fire ammo, and out to 145 yards with our 40-grain CCI Green Tag .22 Long Rifle load, which has become the go-to rimfire bullet for our test because of its consistency and affordability.

​It’s interesting to observe that the Crossfire HD scopes are only available in MOA configurations, and Vortex has leaned heavily into its Dead-Hold 2A BDC reticle. For shooters and hunters who want Vortex scopes with MIL references or first focal-plane systems, the Diamondback and Strike Eagle families have more options.

​The glass in the Crossfire HD line is underwhelming, with a milky veil and some edge distortion. The eyebox in the 6-18 is pretty stingy, and with no manual parallax correction, the focus on the 3-9×40 version is glitchy, with testers complaining that they could rarely keep both the reticle and the target in the same focal plane.

​Also of note: the 2-8×32 Crossfire HD was one of the few scopes to struggle in our drop test. After three drops from 36 inches on a pad, the scope’s zero shifted about 2 MOA at 50 yards. The 3-9×40 version passed the drop test with no detectable zero shift.

​Durability certainly matters, but we reckoned that hunters aren’t buying these scopes for the glass; they’re buying them because they represent a raging value for all-around hunting optics. Turrets tracked well. Controls are generally smooth and precise. And for scopes costing under $300, the Crossfire HD line earned mammoth price/value scores.

​“Easy, simple, hunting budget workhorse,” notes tester Todd Netto. “I think your average hunter would take this over the other $300 scopes in our test. It’s worth that price all day long.”

Andrew McKean is Outdoor Life’s hunting and conservation editor, drilling into issues that affect wildlife, wildlands, and the people who care about them. He’s also OL’s optics editor, helping readers to make informed buying decisions.

Liverpool looking at one of Ruben Amorim’s top players at AC Milan – report

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Liverpool are reportedly paying close attention to AC Milan centre-back Koni De Winter.

That’s the word coming from CaughtOffside’s Mark Brus on The Daily Briefing, with Manchester United and Chelsea also said to be among the defender’s potential suitors.

The three big-hitters in question aren’t the only Premier League clubs keeping a watching brief on the No.5.

So there’s a distinct possibility that De Winter could be making his move to the English top-flight sooner rather than later, if he can maintain his current performance levels.

From Liverpool’s perspective, interest in signing a new centre-back is more than understandable given question marks over the futures of Joe Gomez and Virgil van Dijk. Meanwhile, it’s not quite clear whether loan signing Ronald Araujo’s move to Anfield will be made permanent in light of his superb form in the 2026/27 season.

Either way, the Reds will require at least one new centre-half come the opening of the summer transfer window.

One sticking point for Liverpool might be Milan and Ruben Amorim’s use of the Belgian star in a three-man central defence.

But looking past that, De Winter has posted some reasonable numbers since his move to the Italian giants last year.

It will certainly be encouraging for Andoni Iraola that the young centre-half ranks well for line-breaking passes and aerial duels, particularly with the club’s progressive capabilities yet to fully recover in a post-Trent Alexander-Arnold world.

* Koni De Winter’s stats for AC Milan in the 2025/26 Serie A season (FotMob)

At 24 years of age, however, it would seem that the former Juventus star is still very much a work in progress. Of course, this will no doubt be welcomed by a coach with Iraola’s appetite for development.

And adding another centre-back option to the ranks who’s capable of getting the ball out of the backline is something we’re far from opposed to.

Earth's Gravity May Have Kept the Early Moon's Volcanic Heart Beating

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

We typically think of the Moon as a static, floating object that has remained the same for basically its entire lifetime, with an occasional asteroid impact serving as an exception. That understanding is grounded in physics - the Moon should have cooled rapidly after forming when a huge protoplanet hit the early Earth around 4.5 billion years ago. But, some lunar material returned from the Changâe-5 mission was notably formed only around two billion years ago, yet appeared to be volcanic in origin. So how did a world that was supposed to have settled down without volcanism remain hot enough to create these materials more than two billion years after its violent birth? A new paper from Miao Chen of the Chengdu University of Technology and his colleagues, and recently published in the journal Planet, offers an explanation - tidal heating.

Letâs first set the stage of the Moonâs creation. Our best guess is that it was created when another early protoplanet, named Theia, slammed into Earth in a massive collision, creating a chunk of debris that coalesced into the Moon. However, that amount of debris was relatively small - only about a quarter the size of the Earth itself, which meant the internal heat generated during its formation would rapidly radiate away, turning the Moon itself geologically dormant.

But when Changâe-5 returned some lunar basalts from Oceanus Procellarum back to Earth, laboratory dating showed them to be only around two billion years old. Basalts are only formed in active volcanoes, or other environments with similarly high pressures and temperatures. Environments that, at least according to traditional geological theory, shouldnât have existed on the Moon two billion years ago.

Fraser explains why the Moon is moving farther away.

Despite that, scientists have tried offering other solutions. Radioactive elements, such as potassium and uranium, emit some heat as they decay. But there wasnât enough un-decayed material left on the Moon to offer the amount of heat needed to form basalt two billion years ago. Asteroid strikes could cause localized high impact thermal spikes, but the heavy bombardment period that gave the Moon most of its craters had ended long before the basalt was formed.

So instead the authors turned to gravity - specifically to a phenomenon called tidal heating. This happens when a celestial body is squeezed, stretched, and kneaded as it travels in an elliptical orbit around a larger parent body. Perhaps the most famous example of this in the solar system is Io. This moon of Jupiter is constantly erupting due to the tidal squishing it undergoes during its orbit around the largest planet.

Obviously, the Moon doesnât seem to be undergoing any such squishing currently. However, two billion years ago, the Moon was much closer to the Earth than it is today, orbiting our planet in sixteen days rather than the twenty seven it takes now. Its orbit was also much more eccentric, looking much more like an oval than a circle. All of which means that Earth itself exerted huge rhythmic tidal forces on the Moon' s interior during the course of its orbit.

Fraser details Tidal Locking, which also affects the Moons geology.

Weâve known that for awhile, but astronomers have always modeled the Moon as a mostly uniform sphere when trying to understand how these gravitational squeezes affected it. However, scientists knew this was a simplification - there is an ultra-low viscosity zone of rock wrapping itself between the Moonâs core and mantle. When the research team plugged a more realistic 3D representation of the mantle setup into a convection simulation, they noticed that a ton of energy was directed specifically at two symmetric regions along the lunar core-mantle boundary.

These regions, which they named Tidal Heating Anomaly Zones (THAZs) , absorbed a huge amount of energy. They calculated that, around 3.2 billion years ago, despite taking up only 2.5% of the mantleâs total volume, the tidal strain on these regions delivered 150 gigawatts of power - more than four times the amount of energy generated by radioactive decay.

This seemed to be the driving force behind much of the Moonâs geological history. It helped keep the lunar core warm, which helped sustain a magnetic field for much longer the models originally predicted (and was a puzzle when scientists realized that Apollo lunar samples seemed to have formed in a strong magnetic field). It also drove huge mantle plumes that swelled toward the crust. Billions of years later, long after the tidal engine has calmed down, those stranded pockets of mantle melted to produce the basalts seen in the Changâe-5 samples.

If thereâs one thing thatâs clear from the paperâs theory, it's that we still donât know a lot about the Moonâs geological history. This new model offers some elegant answers to some of the most persistent questions in that area, but as of now itâs still only a theory. Maybe some of the numerous lunar missions planned over the next decade will be able to provide more definitive data to finally solve the evolutionary questions of our nearest neighbor.

Andy has been interested in space exploration ever since reading Pale Blue Dot in middle school. An engineer by training, he likes to focus on the practical challenges of space exploration, whether that's getting rid of perchlorates on Mars or making ultra-smooth mirrors to capture ever clearer data. When not writing or engineering things he can be found entertaining his four children, four cats, and two dogs, or running in circles and throwing plastic discs at people to stay in shape.

Which is the best iPad to buy right now? After 16 years of using them, this is our take

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Join The Club for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation, and sign you up to our newsletter.

Discover whether you need a basic iPad, Air, Pro or mini.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Choosing an iPad used to be a piece of cake. Now there are four families, different processors, and prices that jumped up in June 2026. So it’s important to pick the right one, and not pay through the nose for power you don't actually need.

The good news is, for browsing, streaming, gaming and a bit of sketching, the classic 11-inch iPad (2025) does everything you probably need. If you have specific tasks beyond that, it might be worth shelling out for a more expensive iPad Air, iPad Pro and iPad mini. But for most people (including me) this is the best iPad to get.

Below, I’ve compared the latest versions of all four models – because they are the best versions of all the families – drawing on both my own experience and reviews by the Creative Bloq team, which has been using iPads since Gen 1 in 2010.

Our top pick for most people is what people often call the basic iPad. But don't let that put you off, because "basic" is purely a relative term here. So yes, this is the cheapest iPad Apple sells, but it’s still a lovely tablet to use every day. Web browsing, streaming, video calls, note-taking and email are all fast and easy, and the bright, colourful screen is big enough to be comfortable without weighing you down.

It’s a decent gaming tablet too, as long as you're not set on playing AAA titles at the highest frame rates. Call of Duty Mobile runs okay, for instance, until you go up to 120fps mode, while Infinity Nikki might need dropping to medium. The lack of an M-series chip also locks you out of some games entirely.

For drawing, though, it does the job nicely. Add an Apple Pencil and you can sketch happily in Procreate, although the first-generation Pencil is the one to get, as the cheaper USB-C model doesn’t have pressure sensitivity. As our reviewer Ian Evenden says: “You don’t get desktop-level processing power, but you do get one of the friendliest, most useful tablets around, especially if you combine it with a Pencil for sketching.”

A couple more things to point out: there’s no Apple Intelligence, the USB-C port is slow and the screen has an air gap beneath the glass, so it feels a little less refined under a stylus. In short, the compromises are there... but they won't be deal-breakers for most people. Finally, bear in mind that reports suggest a replacement could arrive in early 2027 – although Apple hasn’t confirmed anything so that's currently just speculation.

The Air costs $300 / £320 more than the basic iPad. In return you get a much faster chip, twice the memory, Apple Intelligence, Apple Pencil Pro support and the option of a 13-inch screen.

That extra power is overkill if all you want to do on your iPad is check emails and watch Netflix. But if you want to run demanding creative software or games, then it may be worth it. In our Geekbench 6 tests, the M4 Air’s multi-core score of 13,168 was more than double the 6,338 we got from the basic iPad. Its graphics score of 53,071 also matched the 53,252 of the previous M4 iPad Pro.

That will really make a difference, for example, if you want to run 3D software. Our reviever, Glen Southern, who has used iPad Pros for professional work since they launched, found that he could sculpt models of up to 16 million polygons in Nomad Sculpt. But he cautions that for some, all that power will be overkill. “For a lot of creative users, 12GB of memory and an M4 chip may be more iPad than they’ll ever actually need,” he says.

On the other side of the equation, the Air isn't quite as capable as the Pro. For instance, it has an LCD screen rather than OLED, and a 60Hz refresh rate rather than 120Hz. For many people, though, that line won’t be worth paying to cross.

The Pro is the absolute best tablet Apple makes, and so if price is no object then please, knock yourself out. It is very expensive, though, and for most people, it’ll be total overkill. However, if you have a specific work need for a Pro such as 3D modelling or video editing, then it may be worth the investment. And it truly is a lovely thing to have and use.

The tandem OLED screen is the best on any iPad, with deep blacks, high contrast and a 120Hz refresh rate. And it’s the most powerful iPad by a distance, scoring 16,443 for multi-core and 74,046 for graphics in our Geekbench 6 tests. “Whether it’s for building up a lot of layers in Procreate or Illustrator, video editing in Final Cut Pro or Davinci Resolve or crazy stuff in Zbrush or Nomad Sculpt, the M5 iPad Pro barely breaks a sweat,” says our reviewer Ian Evenden.

That price, though, is not to be sneezed at. And the cost climbs quickly once you add a Magic Keyboard, Apple Pencil Pro and extra storage. So unless you're certain exactly what the Pro would do for you, we'd say: get the Air.

The iPad mini is, just as the name suggests, small and light. That makes it a great choice if you want something you can carry easily in a small bag. It's also useful for using in tight spaces, like a crowded railway carriage.

If you’re a fan of small, and you want the best, the 2026 version is the one to choose. It's the first mini with an OLED screen, the first with IP67 water and dust resistance, and at 255g, it’s noticeably lighter than its predecessor.

We haven’t tested it ourselves yet, but our sister site TechRadar praised both its screen and fast performance. So if you want an iPad for reading, travel, note-taking and sketching on the go, it's a great choice; especially if you opt for the paper-like nano-texture screen, which costs an extra $100 / £100 on the 256GB and 512GB models.

The catch? It's a whopping $250 / £270 more than the basic iPad and only $50 / £50 less than the 11-inch Air, so you really are paying a premium for the smallness. The 8.5-inch screen is also stuck at 60Hz. However, if you want to save some cash and the upgrades aren't important to you, retailers still have the previous A17 Pro model, which has dropped to a record low.

The best iPad overall is the iPad Pro, which is number three on our list above. It's the fastest, most powerful and has the best screen. So if money's no object, this the one you should buy, unless you're mainly interested in something small and portable (in which case, go for the iPad mini, number four on our list.) However, if you don't have endless cash to spare, the iPad Pro may well be overkill, so it's worth deciding whether you really need all that power. The basic iPad is much cheaper and, as discussed below, is actually the best pick for most people.

The basic iPad (which is just called "iPad" on Apple's website and retailer listings) is the one most people should buy. It’s by far the cheapest way to get a new iPad, and handles browsing, streaming, email, video calls and casual gaming with ease. If you don't want to do anything more complicated with an iPad, there's no reason to pay more. It's number one on our list above.

The basic iPad, number one on our list above, is the best choice for most students, if you just want it for low-powered tasks like taking notes, reading, checking emails, listening to music and watching video. If you study a creative subject or use demanding apps, though, the iPad Air is worth the extra money if your budget allows.

The basic iPad with an Apple Pencil is the affordable way to start with drawing on the iPad. If you’d like a better drawing experience, the Air gives you a laminated screen, Apple Pencil Pro support and the option of a 13-inch canvas. For more detail, see our guide to the best iPads for drawing.

The iPad Pro, number 3 on our list, is the one to get if you need its OLED screen, 120Hz refresh rate or maximum power. But check that you really do. Many professionals will be just as happy with the 13-inch Air. Our guide to the best iPad for Procreate goes into more detail.

I Found $1.70 Burritos At Denver Airport. Thank You, Taco Bell.

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

While I usually just make a beeline for the Capital One Lounge while in Denver International Airport, I recently noticed that Taco Bell is back…now airside instead of landside..and from a cost perspective, probably the best deal for food in DEN.

If you’ve flown from Denver for years, you might remember that there used to be a Taco Bell in the Jeppesen Terminal in the food court above the security checkpoint. At some point that closed while the airport was refurbishing that area and did not re-open.

But was I (very pleasantly) surprised to see a Taco Bell Cantina in Concourse A (near Gates A47 and A49) during a recent trip through DEN. I had no idea!

Even better? The prices were street pricing, not airport pricing. $1.70 for a burrito? What a lovely way to get your junk food fix without breaking the bank.

And street pricing not ubiquitous in the airport. I had a salad with some pulled brisket at Brothers BBQ and it was almost $20….

I also want to note that I tried the more “local” option, called Denver Street Eats, located in The B Gates, which offers street tacos. A steak taco was $6.25. That may seem reasonable at first glance, but it was just a small flour tortilla with very little to it: a bit of steak, a couple spinach leaves, and a few tiny slices of picked onions. It was good, but sorry, Taco Bell is better.

So if you’re looking for an affordable meal at Denver Airport, Taco Bell is your best bet.

And for new readers, yes, I have a strong affinity for Taco Bell…wherever I am in the world. Check out the archive.

World of Warcraft: Forever hotfix puts a stop to super speedy flag carriers that were dominating PvP battlegrounds

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

World of Warcraft: Forever has received its first major hotfix patch, which among other things has put an end to an especially powerful bug that made PvP battlegrounds like Warsong Gulch favoured to the Horde's advantage.

While Taurens are running, they gradually build up stacks of increased movement speed. They gain a two percent movement speed bonus per stack, and it stacks up to 30 times. That means a Tauren can gain 60 percent increased movement speed, though it's worth noting that when they stop moving, these stacks decrease. In short, cows run fast.

The Hotfix, detailed in this Blizzard blog post, stops the Tauren's plainsrunning passive from stacking during a battleground's preparation phase. What players were doing to exploit this was running back and forth in the minutes before the Warsong Gulch battleground let both teams loose. It's a capture the flag game mode, so once the gates opened, Taurens were sprinting ahead of everyone else, allowing them to quickly push towards the enemy base for a potential early flag capture.

In addition, the hotfix removes any plainsrunning stacks from Taurens once they pick up the enemy flag, and prevents them from gaining stacks while the flag is in their possession. The goal of the enemy team is to stop and kill the enemy flag carrier as soon as possible, so a Tauren moving far faster than any of your team made this pretty difficult.

Tauren's could be tanky warriors or druids too, which further added to the difficulty in killing them. With this change they're now easier to lock down, though it's worth noting a Tauren can still make use of Plainsrunning to rush into the enemy base early. They just don't have that wild speed boost from the jump.

Surely this was the only reason my Alliance Warsong Gulch games were all lost this morning, and not because my faction is for chumps. In other World of Warcraft: Forever news, the launch earlier this week was applauded as one of the smoothest in the series history, save for a few broken boats and zeppelins.

The hotfix has also decreased the respawn time of the NPC Deathstalker Erland who is needed for an escort quest. People were mulling around his spawn and wasting away while waiting for him to respawn. Also, supply vendors on the Zephras Isle have been stocked with handy tradeskill bags that you could find in other starter zones. It's the shipping I assume, it takes a while for stock to make it all the way up there.

Tourism Heads to COP31 Wanting Results, Not More Pledges

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Enter your email for a complimentary article + exclusive offers.

Photo Credit: UN Climate Change - Kiara Worth

Five years after the Glasgow Declaration, tourism has plenty of climate commitments but little shared proof of what they’ve achieved. The industry is still split on whether better measurement or faster action should come first.

COP31, the world’s annual marathon of climate talks, kicks off next week. After years of planning climate action, tourism operators told Skift that they now want to see the receipts.

The 31st United Nations Climate Change Conference will take place in Antalya, Türkiye, from November 9 to 20. Co-hosted with Australia, this fresh round of climate talks is being billed the implementation COP, after parties ultimately failed to agree on a path forward last year in Belém, Brazil.

The Brazilian COP30 presidency promised to publish plans for moving away from fossil fuels and protecting forests before the next round of talks.

The voluntary roadmaps, released Tuesday, are designed to help policymakers implement their existing climate commitments through national policies, investments and measurable results — and should indicate some of the focus of the forthcoming talks.

The newly published fossil fuel roadmap, building on the

Tags: climate change, cop31, extreme weather, Glasgow Declaration, greenshift, policy, sustainability, un tourism

Skift provides the insights. You make the decisions.

Decision intelligence for travel leaders.

COP31 opens in Antalya, Türkiye, from November 9 to 20, co-hosted with Australia and billed as the "implementation COP" after parties failed to agree on a path forward at COP30 in Belém. Tourism gets a thematic slot on November 18 for the third year running, this time inside a day on human and social development, and the sustainability executives Skift spoke with said they want evidence of change rather than new pledges. Radisson Hotel Group's Sven Wiltink pointed to inconsistent measurement of emissions, water, energy, and waste, arguing that without common metrics "comparisons are meaningless and claims remain unverifiable." Intrepid's Susanne Etti said the sector needs electrification and falling emissions, not more commitments, targets, or frameworks, and that resilience, adaptation, and loss and damage funding have become more urgent after a year of extreme heat, wildfires, and flooding. Booking Holdings' Danielle D'Silva said the industry can do more on both adaptation and emissions reductions. COP31 also marks five years since the Glasgow Declaration on Climate Action in Tourism, which has drawn more than 900 signatories; revised operational guidelines will be released at the conference, with co-author Jeremy Smith saying the aim is to lower the measurement and reporting burden and bring in companies that stayed out.

This 100-Year-Old Louisiana Home Got a New Plot of Land and a Colorful New Chapter

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

The expansive property also includes a pool house and carriage house.

Long before it became the backdrop for family dinners, afternoon swims, and spirited tennis matches, this century-old Colonial in Baton Rouge, Louisiana, had already lived a rather extraordinary life. In the late 1920s, the house was dismantled and relocated from Prairieville to its current address, where its distinctive red clay roof tiles—salvaged from Louisiana State University—have presided over a landscape of majestic live oaks ever since. Decades later, a young girl growing up down the street would admire the house, never imagining that one day she’d raise a family of her own beneath that very roof.

When she and her husband finally purchased the property, they knew that turning a beloved neighborhood landmark into a forever home for themselves and their three daughters would require a little imagination—and a lot of careful planning. Enter interior designer Anne Underwood, who was tasked with bringing the historic residence into the present while preserving the quirks that made it so beloved. Over several years, the project grew from a renovation of the original four-bedroom home into a sprawling family compound, complete with a carriage house, pool house, and landscaped grounds designed to make an ordinary weekend feel like a vacation.

“While they loved the historic feel and comfortable size of the main home for their family, they needed extra space to relax and entertain, and privacy to host guests,” explains Underwood. “We planned for what they need now but also anticipated the future.”

Inside, Underwood embraced the home’s traditional bones without letting them stiffen the home (and the family’s) sunny disposition. Lemon-yellow checkerboard floors mingle with antique furnishings (an element Underwood says is a must for creating “a lived-in, collected-over-time feel that I find crucial to making a true home”), hand-painted finishes, and exuberant textiles. A whimsical chandelier welcomes guests into the living room, while a tucked-away banquette has become an unofficial headquarters for homework, puzzles, and art projects.

Outside, the property’s newest additions—including a pool house and carriage house—offer an equally playful proposition. Conceived as the estate’s crown jewel, the pool house features pale wood walls, a colorful handwoven light fixture, and a monumental wood-burning pizza oven. The homeowners’ relatives jokingly dubbed the expanding property their own private resort, a description Underwood happily embraces.

“So much was poured into this project to make it unique, joyful, and really functional for the clients,” says Underwood. “The warmth and care of each detailed layer creates a sense of ease, which is what facilitates the joyfulness that plays out inside and outside these walls.”

FAST FACTSDesigner: Anne UnderwoodArchitect: LRK ArchitectsContractor: Bernhardt Normand ConstructionStyling: Lucy BammanLandscape Architect: ARBO Landscape Architects Location: Baton Rouge, LouisianaThe Space: A four-bedroom, three-and-a-half-bathroom main house, with a carriage house and pool house.

With a simpler architectural structure, the pool house was a place where Underwood leaned into the family’s playful energy. “We selected the wall stain to look like the inside of a shell and wanted the furnishings to feel layered, colorful, vibrant—like a vacation but also very durable for wet swimsuits and sweaty tennis players!” she adds.

The first selection for the pool house was also its most exuberant: a 12-light PET Lamp fixture, with each colorful pendant and cord individually customized and positioned beneath the soaring vaulted ceiling. “Its colorful, playful nature was exactly what we wanted and is very effective for balancing light throughout the space with high vaulted ceilings,” says Underwood. Below, a generous sectional upholstered in indoor-outdoor fabric creates an inviting place to retreat after a swim.

With vibrant Moroccan mosaic flooring, blue zellige shower tile, and a vintage wooden mirror, the pool house bathroom could easily belong to a boutique hotel on some far-flung island. Wood siding and thoughtfully chosen antique details lend the space a relaxed, resort-inspired character that feels right at home beside the pool.

Tradition gets a playful twist in the home’s primary gathering space, where a hand-knotted rug and classic upholstered furnishings mingle with an exuberant chandelier by Stray Dog Designs.

“This house lacks a foyer, so we wanted the living room to be welcoming with plenty of seating and layers of color and accessories,” says Underwood. “While this is the more ‘formal’ living space, we selected a bright, whimsical light fixture to let guests know that these homeowners don’t take themselves (or their interiors) too seriously.”

A passageway between the living room and the home’s more casual quarters, the dining room needed to make an impression without feeling overly precious. Schumacher’s Weeping Pine wallpaper wraps the space in pattern, while pale blue trim, upholstered chairs, and vintage sconces sourced from Round Top lend the formal setting an unexpected sense of ease.

With the adjoining breakfast room to consider, Underwood gave the kitchen a quieter disposition. Existing cabinetry received a custom pale green stain, expertly applied by a local faux-finish artist, while paneled appliances and tonal counter stools create a seamless effect. The yellow-and-white checkerboard flooring continues underfoot, connecting the two spaces.

A powder room is as good a place as any to indulge a dramatic streak. “We wanted a bold escape for guests in the powder bath, and this incredible grasscloth wallpaper was the jumping-off point,” says Underwood. She complemented the Aux Abris pattern’s lush, atmospheric tone with a wood-grained antique chest repurposed as a marble-topped vanity. Vintage Murano glass sconces and a striped inlay mirror add a final dose of eccentricity.

In the office, Underwood leaned into the appeal of a well-appointed study, furnishing this hardworking space almost entirely with vintage and antique finds. Custom cabinetry painted in Benjamin Moore’s Templeton Gray conceals printers, files, and other less-than-picturesque necessities, while mohair-upholstered armchairs make the space equally suited to work and contemplation.

As it turns out, the inspiration for the primary suite was waiting just outside the window. “I started with the idea of bringing materials—like the patinated copper on all exterior lanterns and gutters—from the exterior to the interior,” says Underwood. “While the walls are painted a green/blue color reminiscent of the patina of weathered copper, the accents in the room are warm shades of camel and copper.”

A Gustavian antique secretary and dimensional papier-maché art from Gusto complete the room's quietly collected atmosphere.

The generously proportioned soaking tub in the primary bathroom has already lived several lives, first as a favorite bathing spot for the family’s three young daughters, and now as a welcome retreat for their parents. Underwood carried the bedroom’s blue-green palette into the bath, introducing blush-patterned wallpaper, graphic stone tile, and black accents to balance the room’s softer flourishes.

In daughter Emily’s room, Underwood chose to embrace the sloped ceiling, outfitting it with custom bed canopies that create an enveloping, almost storybook atmosphere. Layers of patterned bedding, including bespoke eiderdowns made in the UK, heighten the cozy effect, while chunky upholstered ottomans offer a welcome counterpoint to the room’s more feminine details.

Making sense of the home’s quirky upstairs architecture required some clever spatial maneuvering. Underwood divided one large bathroom into two separate spaces, each with its own dressing vanity. In daughter Olivia’s bath, Salvesen Graham floral wallpaper covers the sloped walls and ceiling, visually softening the room’s unusual angles. “The pattern play in the tile, wallpaper, and fabrics keeps the energy moving throughout the space,” she adds.

In the carriage house, Underwood continued the layered approach with velvet, chenille, and a generously proportioned sectional, creating a retreat where overnight guests can comfortably settle in for an afternoon—or an entire weekend.

The carriage house kitchen offers guests all the comforts of home, with a nostalgic twist. Cabinetry painted Benjamin Moore’s Mistletoe features vintage-inspired vent detailing, while a retro SMEG refrigerator reinforces the room’s charming, slightly old-fashioned character.

Underwood preserved the home’s original red clay roof tiles while refreshing its facade using Benjamin Moore’s White Dov on the siding and Kennebunkport Green on the shutters. An expanded bluestone sidewalk gives the front door prominence while mature live oaks lend the property a sense of permanence that no renovation could replicate.

Anne Underwood is the founder of Underwood Interiors. Known for her thoughtful approach to creating layered, Anne creates livable spaces that balance timeless tradition with a fresh, playful sensibility. With an appreciation for antiques, vintage furnishings, and artisanal craftsmanship, her interiors feel collected over time rather than decorated all at once. Her work celebrates color, pattern, and architectural character, with an emphasis on thoughtful details and everyday functionality. Whether reimagining a historic residence or designing a new space from the ground up, she believes the most successful interiors reflect the personalities of the people who call them home.

We've been covering Black Friday iPad deals for as long as I can remember (12 solid years to be exact!), and during all the time I've been involved, I've been a champion for the mid-tier iPad Air.

But annually increasing capabilities, and largely a price freeze, has meant that it's no longer my idea of the best iPad around anymore. That title now goes to the (not really that) humble iPad. The A16, 2025 version, to be precise.

The iPad Pro is my least favourite iPad, and that's because I'm not a video editor or professional digital artists, so I have no need for its immense power. Grow These 6 Beautiful Living Christmas Trees in Pots – Then Enjoy Them in Your Garden for Years

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Join The Club for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation, and sign you up to our newsletter.

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Beautiful plants and ideas to brighten your garden.

Expert tips for growing fruit, vegetables and herbs at home.

Sign up to get fresh gardening ideas, inspiration and expert advice delivered straight to your inbox.

What Your Christmas Cactus Needs in November – Ace These 4 Tasks for Fabulous Holiday Flowers

7 Foolproof Plants Anyone Can Propagate From Cuttings in November – Grow More of Your Favorite Shrubs for Free

8 Easy Landscape Designer Tricks to Transition Your Garden From Fall into Winter

Sign up for the Gardening Know How newsletter today and receive a free copy of our e-book "How to Grow Delicious Tomatoes".

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

A real Christmas tree doesn't have to be disposable. Choose the right variety, and your festive centerpiece can have a whole new life outdoors.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

If there is one piece of decor that represents the holidays, it’s got to be a Christmas tree. Add an evergreen tree to your living room, toss on some tinsel, and, suddenly, it’s the holidays.

But Christmas trees seem more expensive every year, and it’s not fun to spend all that money on a one-use tree that you'll discard in January. This is the year to switch to a beautiful live Christmas tree that lasts for years. There are lots of miniature Christmas-type trees that can be grown in pots, spruced up for the holiday, then enjoyed in the garden for the rest of the year.

The key is to buy a living tree that is attractive and built for the long haul, not a single use. I’ve got you covered on that score with a short list of the best living Christmas tree options, from miniature traditional-looking spruces to unusual blue and golden conifers.

What’s the best small evergreen tree to grow in a pot for Christmas? Well, the answer will be different for each of us. There are plenty of attractive trees that make good live Christmas trees through December, then live happily in the garden the rest of the year. Pick your favorite, but make sure it’s suited to your USDA hardiness zone so it can thrive outdoors for years to come!

Dwarf Alberta spruce (Picea glauca 'Conica') makes wonderful live, potted Christmas trees. These trees offer a classic pyramidal Christmas tree shape and incredible longevity – to 50 years or more in the right conditions. Their compact, densely packed growth habit makes these trees look cute, and they grow very slowly – between 2 and 4 inches (2.5–5 cm) per year – and top out at just over 10 feet (3 m) tall and wide. The needles are fine-textured and soft to the touch, a rich blue-green. They are also aromatic when rubbed.

You can order a classic dwarf Alberta spruce online at Nature Hills, or for something a little different, the Rainbow's End cultivar, which produces a second flush of lemon-yellow growth in summer.

These little spruces are easy to care for, drought-tolerant, and cold-tolerant. They grow best in well-drained acidic soil and prefer full sun. Only water when the top 3 inches of soil are dry. Their preferred climate (USDA zones 2–8) has cold winters and cool summers.

Sign up for the Gardening Know How newsletter today and receive a free copy of our e-book "How to Grow Delicious Tomatoes".

A dwarf blue spruce (Picea pungens 'Globosa') can make a great compact, living Christmas tree. You can doll it up for the holidays indoors, then move this slow-growing evergreen conifer outdoors for the winter in USDA zones 2–8. It needs little maintenance and offers a tidy, manageable size and striking silver-blue foliage.

The signature blue needles make the dwarf spruce a unique holiday tree, and its strong, stiff branches hold up heavy ornaments without issue. The natural pyramidal form requires little shaping, and its needles rarely fall out. On the other hand, the needles are prickly and can hurt you when you are decorating if you aren’t careful. Plus, this spruce doesn’t offer the beloved evergreen aroma, but a distinct odor that isn’t universally loved.

Dwarf blue spruce trees require very little attention once established. When you are moving the tree outside, put it in a site with lots of direct sun. Add several inches of organic mulch to the soil. The ‘Globosa’ cultivar, available from Woodies Garden Goods, reaches 3 to 5 feet (1–1.5 m) tall and wide.

Korean fir (Abies koreana) is a compact, slow-growing, symmetrical evergreen tree. With its soft, rounded needles and dense growth habit, it is a popular choice for a live Christmas tree.

The needles are very ornamental, a dark green with a silvery underside, and offer a rich, fresh forest scent with notes of citrus. The cones are special too, a striking violet-purple and stand upright. This slow-growing fir creeps up a bit every year to finish at some 15-30 feet tall, with a conical form. A species tree will grow to 30 feet (10 m) tall, but a dwarf variety like Cis Dwarf Korean Fir, from Nature Hills, stops at 2 to 3 feet tall and wide. For something different, Ice Breaker Korean Fir is a dwarf variety that looks perpetually snow-covered.

Plant in full sun to partial shade in moist, well-drained, acidic soil. These trees thrive in USDA zones 5–7.

A dwarf Norway spruce (Picea abies) makes a charming, compact live Christmas tree that can be kept in a pot or, in zones 3–7, planted in the garden after the holidays. These small trees typically reach around 3 feet (1 m) tall and wide at maturity, depending on the cultivar. They make charming, sustainable live Christmas trees and can be moved outdoors after the festive season.

A dwarf Norway spruce tree offers a classic triangular shape, dark green needles, and graceful, drooping branchlets. This classic Christmas tree is popular for its dense, symmetrical shape and rich "Christmassy" scent that quickly fills up the room. Unfortunately, Norway spruce needles can dry out and drop fast in warm indoor temperatures, so keep it in as cool a spot as possible.

These trees are generally about 3 feet tall (90 cm) when sold as potted Christmas trees, and the ultimate height depends on the cultivar. They grow slowly, adding between 1 and 3 inches (5–10 cm) every year.

You can buy both ‘Pumila’ dwarf Norway spruce and ‘Pusch’ dwarf Norway spruce at Nature Hills. ‘Pumila’ has a dense, bushy shape that looks lovely decorated for Christmas, while ‘Pusch’ offers something a little different, producing tiny red cones in spring that add extra interest to the garden.

Japanese cedars (Cryptomeria japonica) make unique live Christmas trees with soft, fine foliage that is pleasant to touch. The appearance depends on the cultivar, but many compact cultivars offer a naturally dense, rounded shape. The foliage can take on bronze, copper, or bluish-green tones during winter.

For a real statement tree, Black Dragon Japanese cedar, available from Plant Addicts, has a mature height of 8–10 feet (2.5–3 m), or for a smaller tree, the Tansu Dwarf Japanese cedar, from Nature Hills, reaches 3–4 feet (1–1.2 m).

Caring for your live Japanese cedar tree isn't difficult, but you’ll want to limit how much time it spends indoors. Too much time in a warm house can make the tree unhappy. Indoors, keep the tree away from direct heat sources. When it’s time to take the tree outside, start it out in a sheltered spot or an unheated garage for a few days.

Be sure to keep the soil moist. Check it every day. Japanese cedars thrive in full sun to partial shade in USDA zones 5–9 and require well-drained, fertile soil. Standard varieties can grow to 50 feet (15 m) tall if planted in the ground. Dwarf cultivars stay much smaller.

You will recognize the lemon cypress (Cupressus macrocarpa ‘Goldcrest’) – it’s the potted evergreen that you are likely to see in every store just before Christmas as a holiday plant. You can even get one from Walmart. It makes a charming, small Christmas tree with its natural pyramid shape, bright chartreuse foliage, and fresh citrus scent.

The lemon cypress is a versatile evergreen that, once the festive season is done, can be kept in a container on a patio in USDA zones 7–10. Be gentle with it indoors. Use lightweight ornaments since the soft branches can bend or break under heavier decorations. You’ll want to avoid hot lights, too. Use battery-operated micro fairy lights that don’t produce excess heat.

These trees grow slowly and can top out at 10 feet (3 m) tall and 3 feet (1 m) wide. Indoors, give them a sunny window with 6 hours of bright light a day. Keep the tree far from hot air vents and cool drafts, and provide a pebble tray for humidity. Transition the cypress to an outdoor location after the holidays.

Teo Spengler is a master gardener and a docent at the San Francisco Botanical Garden, where she hosts public tours. She has studied horticulture and written about nature, trees, plants and gardening for more than two decades. Before turning her focus to gardening, she worked as an attorney and legal writer, experience she also brings to her role as our Gardening Lawyer. Her extended family includes some 30 houseplants and hundreds of outdoor plants, including 250 trees, which are her main passion. Spengler currently splits her life between San Francisco and the French Basque Country, though she was raised in Alaska, giving her experience of gardening in a range of climates.

Democrats won control of Congress. So what does that mean for the Supreme Court?

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Type to search articles, cases, and authors.

Democrats won big in Tuesday’s midterm elections, flipping at least 40 seats in the House and at least seven in the Senate. Come January, the Democratic Party will control both houses of Congress, which will allow it to set policymaking priorities and play a lead role in shaping the future of the Supreme Court.

Most immediately, the power shift in the Senate will complicate (if not effectively eliminate) President Donald Trump’s ability to appoint another justice to the Supreme Court, should a vacancy arise after the new Congress is seated. The Democratic Party will have enough votes to reject a nomination – if it even allows confirmation hearings to proceed.

Kelsey Dallas is SCOTUSblog's managing editor and the primary author of the SCOTUStoday newsletter.

Here's What The Expanse: Osiris Reborn's Recast Main Character Looks and Sounds Like

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Developer and publisher Owlcat Games has revealed the new face and voice of the male protagonist in The Expanse: Osiris Reborn.

Following negative feedback from fans, the company announced it would be recasting the role and making updates to the character's appearance.

In the below video, the revamped captain makes his debut:

The new voice is provided by actor Blythe Melin, who will perform as both the male protagonist and his brother, J.

The video also shows a quite dramatic visual change for the character's default look. Players will be able to fully customise their protagonist, but Owlcat says in a press release it's "important to have a default look players can use as a basis for their own captain."

"The developers have redesigned the male captain’s model to give him a less standard military personnel design with more detail and polish for players to work with. His handsome appearance is also matched by his new voice, brought to life by voice actor Blythe Melin, who previously worked on titles like Genshin Impact, Honkai Star Rail, Diablo IV, Ready or Not, and more. Blythe doesn’t just take over as the captain, he also voices his more carefree and relaxed identical twin J."

Owlcat adds that more information about additional members of the cast will be shared "at a later date."

As mentioned, the original version of the male captain was widely panned, a criticism we made in our hands-on preview earlier this year.

Early responses to the changes appear to be pretty positive, though, which is good news.
