The $50 Million Gamble That Made Red Bull Marketing History

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Hi, I'm Yukti Sachan, a writer and content creator from India with an interest in storytelling, books, marketing, and formula 1

Hi, I'm Yukti Sachan, a writer and content creator from India with an interest in storytelling, books, marketing, and formula 1

Hi, I'm Yukti Sachan, a writer and content creator from India with an interest in storytelling, books, marketing, and formula 1

Imagine someone asks you, “Hey, do you want to jump from the edge of space?”

Your first response would probably be, “What the hell is wrong with you?”

But when that exact question was asked to Felix Baumgartner, he basically went, “Yeah, sure. Why not?”

Now, you might be wondering who on Earth was crazy enough to ask that question in the first place.

Ladies and gentlemen, this is the story of the Red Bull Stratos mission: a man crazy enough to jump from the edge of space, a company crazy enough to fund it, and a PR gamble that somehow paid off spectacularly.

Quick disclaimer: Everything you’re about to read comes from me spending the last few hours buried in articles, reports, and marketing resources because apparently that’s what I do for fun now. I tried reading an actual marketing textbook, but it made me question reality, so I fled to Wikipedia, reports, and other resources instead. If I get something wrong, please let me know! Constructive corrections are always welcome—I like learning more than pretending I’m right.

Enough corporate jargon. Let's throw a man out of space.

Now switch the M and the E, and you get Max Emilian Verstappen—okay, that’s not how names work. You get Max Verstappen. But let’s not get distracted.

Earned Media Value (EMV) is the estimated dollar value of publicity a brand receives without paying for it directly.

“The estimated dollar equivalent of unpaid public exposure.”

Which is technically correct, but sounds like a corporate robot wrote it.

So here’s the version my brain understands:

You do something. People can’t stop talking about it. You didn’t pay them to talk about it. That’s EMV.

Influencers posting because they genuinely found something cool? EMV.

People sending the campaign to their friends? EMV.

Yeah... people are definitely going to talk about that.

A simplified formula looks something like this:

EMV = (Total Impressions × Industry CPM) ÷ 1,000

Basically, it estimates how much money the brand would have had to spend on advertising to achieve the same amount of attention.

Some companies also factor in engagement, audience quality, creator influence, and even audience sentiment. After all, ten million people saying, “This campaign is brilliant,” and ten million people saying, “This campaign is terrible,” are two very different outcomes.

Because it lets them estimate the value of all the buzz they generated without paying directly for every conversation.

Imagine your campaign gets millions of people talking. If buying that same amount of attention through traditional advertising would’ve cost millions of dollars, that’s exactly what EMV is trying to estimate.

It isn’t money sitting in Red Bull’s bank account.

It’s simply a way of estimating the advertising value of the attention they earned.

Compared to EMV, ROI is wonderfully straightforward.

ROI simply measures how much profit (or loss) you made compared to how much you invested.

ROI = (Net Profit ÷ Cost of Investment) × 100

Suppose you invest $1,000 into something.

That means your investment generated a 50% return.

A positive ROI means your investment made money.

(Or, as I like to call it, congratulations—you’ve invented a very expensive hobby.)

So what’s the difference between EMV and ROI?

EMV asks: “How valuable was all the attention we earned?”

ROI asks: “Did we actually make money compared to what we spent?”

The other measures financial performance.

We’ll need both of these in a minute because Red Bull Stratos wasn’t just an engineering miracle.

And that's exactly why Red Bull Stratos is still taught as one of the greatest marketing campaigns ever executed.

Somewhere inside Red Bull, on what was probably a completely normal Tuesday, someone had a thought.

A completely normal thought, according to Red Bull standards. Which is already slightly concerning, because Red Bull’s definition of “normal” has historically been questionable.

“Everyone is doing television commercials, right?”

Then came the question that changed everything:

At the time, YouTube livestreaming was still a relatively new idea. Instead of buying a traditional advertising slot, Red Bull could create something people would actively choose to watch.

What could possibly be big enough to capture the world’s attention?

“What if a man jumped from the stratosphere?”

Now, most companies would probably stop here and call it a concerning brainstorming session.

Red Bull looked at the idea and apparently said:

And just like that, Red Bull Stratos was born.

With an estimated investment of around $50 million, Red Bull wasn’t simply funding a stunt. They were funding a scientific mission, an engineering challenge, and one of the boldest marketing gambles in history.

Fifty million dollars is the kind of money that makes normal people rethink buying a coffee.

Red Bull looked at that amount and thought:

“Perfect. Let’s use it to make a human being fall from the sky.”

Unlike certain Formula 1 teams that shall remain unnamed (Ferrari, I’m looking at you), Red Bull actually had a plan.

An Austrian skydiver with years of experience performing extreme jumps, Felix was not exactly someone who looked at a dangerous idea and immediately walked away.

When Red Bull approached him with the idea of jumping from the stratosphere, his reaction was essentially:

Which is either the bravest sentence ever spoken or the sentence responsible for several safety engineers losing sleep.

But this wasn’t just a man standing on a platform and falling.

Behind that jump were scientists, engineers, safety experts, pressure suits, a specially designed capsule, and a massive helium balloon carrying Felix nearly 39 kilometers above Earth.

Basically, hundreds of extremely intelligent people worked together to make sure one person could safely do something that sounded like a rejected cartoon plot.

On October 14, 2012, Felix Baumgartner stepped out of his capsule approximately 39 kilometers above Earth.

During his free fall, he reached a maximum speed of around 1,357 km/h, making him the first person to break the sound barrier during a free fall.

The entire event was livestreamed, from the balloon launch to the moment Felix stepped into the unknown.

This was not a commercial people had to sit through before watching something else.

This was the thing people wanted to watch.

The jump itself lasted around 10 minutes, with approximately 4 minutes and 20 seconds spent in free fall before his parachute opened.

What looked like a man simply falling from the sky was actually years of preparation, engineering, testing, and scientific collaboration.

Red Bull had spent years preparing for a moment that would last only a few minutes.

But those few minutes had the potential to become one of the greatest marketing successes ever created.

Because this was not a pre-recorded commercial where a company could quietly delete the footage and pretend nothing happened.

And the internet, famously known for being calm and reasonable during unexpected events, was waiting.

If something went wrong, millions of people would see it happen in real time.

Red Bull wasn’t just risking $50 million.

They had spent years preparing for a moment that would last only a few minutes.

And then Felix stepped out of the capsule.

At no point during the livestream did Felix stop mid-free fall, pull out a can of Red Bull, take a sip, and say,

“Look at me! I’m literally flying through the stratosphere!”

Because he was wearing a Red Bull-branded pressure suit.

The massive helium balloon carrying him nearly 39 kilometres into the sky?

They didn’t interrupt people’s attention.

Nobody tuned in because they wanted to watch an advertisement.

“Oh my God. Someone is actually going to jump from the edge of space.”

Humans have a fascinating habit of stopping to watch extraordinary things unfold.

When something unbelievable happens, people naturally gather around it.

Instead of asking millions of people to spend thirty seconds watching their advertisement...

...they created something millions of people willingly spent hours watching.

And the brand just happened to own that moment.

Because Red Bull has never really sold caffeine.

They’ve sold the idea that normal is boring.

People don’t buy Red Bull because it’s simply an energy drink.

They buy into everything the brand represents.

Whether that’s Formula 1, cliff diving, mountain biking, air races, or a man casually deciding gravity is optional.

Then the internet did what the internet does best.

Friends sending each other the livestream.

Every conversation became free advertising.

Exactly what we talked about earlier with Earned Media Value.

Nearly eight million people watched simultaneously on YouTube.

Hundreds of millions watched clips afterwards.

The campaign generated an estimated $6 billion in earned media value.

Red Bull didn’t interrupt millions of people to tell them they were exciting.

They did something so exciting that millions of people interrupted their own lives to watch.

How Red Bull Accidentally Built One of the Most Successful Media Empires in Sport

Why Robots Need Intelligence Built for the Physical World

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Jon Stojan is a professional writer based in Wisconsin committed to delivering diverse and exceptional content..

Jon Stojan is a professional writer based in Wisconsin committed to delivering diverse and exceptional content..

Jon Stojan is a professional writer based in Wisconsin committed to delivering diverse and exceptional content..

Robbyant’s LingBot 2.0 starts with the demands of perception and physical action to build intelligence across different robot bodies.

A chatbot can wait for another prompt. A robot may already be moving by the time its surroundings change. That difference turns embodied AI into a distinct engineering problem, where intelligence has to account for physical space and predict how an action will affect what happens next. Robbyant, an embodied AI company under Ant Group, built LingBot 2.0 around those demands from the beginning.

The system reflects a deceptively difficult question in robotics: What does an AI architecture need when its decisions have immediate physical consequences? Robbyant’s answer connects perception, environmental modeling, and execution through six models developed as parts of one embodied-native system.

A robot operating in a home or workplace receives a continuous stream of spatial information. Objects have depth and position, people move around, and the environment can change during a task. Understanding a verbal instruction covers only one piece of that process.

LingBot-Vision and LingBot-Depth 2.0 form the perception side of LingBot 2.0. They give robots information about objects and three-dimensional space that can feed into later reasoning and action. Robbyant describes this layer as “See Clearer,” reflecting the need for physical agents to understand where things are before deciding how to interact with them.

That spatial information also has to remain useful as conditions develop. A robot reaching toward an object needs intelligence capable of working with the environment it encounters at that moment.

The next part of the system addresses prediction. LingBot-Video and LingBot-World 2.0 model physical environments and how they develop over time, creating Robbyant’s “Think Deeper” layer.

This ability has particular significance for robots because movement changes the environment. Picking something up alters its position. Moving through a room creates a new spatial relationship with everything nearby. Effective reasoning involves anticipating what an action is likely to produce.

The models give developers a way to study environmental interaction alongside the perception data that informs it. That relationship is central to Robbyant’s full-stack approach: seeing and predicting supply information that execution can use.

Eventually, the robot has to move. LingBot-VLA 2.0 and LingBot-VA 2.0 handle the “Act Faster” portion of the architecture, translating information from earlier stages into physical behavior.

LingBot-VA 2.0 serves as the capstone of the system. Rather than adapting a model trained for language or entertainment purposes, Robbyant built it from scratch around the demands of embodied AI, with dynamic modeling and causal prediction incorporated into an architecture intended for environmental interaction and real-time execution.Latency becomes especially consequential here. Software generating text can spend additional time producing an answer with few physical consequences. A robot responding to movement around it operates on a different clock. Its perception and reasoning have to reach the action system quickly enough for the response to remain relevant.

Hardware creates another complication: bodies differ. A model working on one physical configuration may eventually need to control machines with different dimensions or capabilities.

Robbyant frames its goal as “building one brain for all robots.” LingBot-VLA 2.0 integrates data from 20 different configurations of robots from 17 different brands during the pre-training phase, enabling cross-embodiment capabilities. LingBot 2.0 as a whole approaches that ambition at the system level, connecting specialized capabilities so embodied intelligence can develop across different robot forms. The six-model architecture gives perception, prediction, and action defined roles within the same technical framework.

Selected models and technologies have also been released as open source, accompanied by technical reports or research papers. That gives developers and researchers material they can inspect and reuse while exploring embodied AI architectures.

The technical challenge of embodied AI begins once intelligence has to exist inside physical space. Every decision occurs amid changing surroundings, and an action can immediately create the conditions for the next one.

LingBot 2.0 treats that cycle as a foundation of robot intelligence. Its six models divide the work across seeing, understanding physical change, and executing movement while remaining connected as a full-stack system. For Robbyant, building a more general robot brain starts with understanding what happens when software has to act in the real world.

25 types of meditation and how to choose the right one

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Clinically reviewed by Dr. Chris Mosunic, PhD, RD, CDCES, MBA

The benefits of meditation can reach across your well-being, from reducing stress and anxiety to improving focus and supporting mental and emotional health. But there’s a catch: meditation is a wide family of practices, and the type that works beautifully for your friend might leave you fidgety and frustrated.

We’ve grouped the many types of meditation into six families, so you can see how they relate before you pick one. Think of it as a menu. Try whichever one draws you in, and let your practice change as you do.

Meditation is a way to train our attention and awareness so we can be more present in the moment. Most types ask you to rest your focus somewhere, like the breath, a word, a sensation, or a movement, and to notice without judgment when your mind drifts, then return. That returning, over and over, is the practice.

What changes from one type to the next is where you place your attention and what you hope to cultivate. Some practices sharpen focus, while others cultivate compassion or help the body relax. Many share a thread of mindfulness, noticing where your attention drifts and gently coming back.

With a history rooted in the spiritual traditions of the East, including Buddhism, Hinduism, and Taoism, meditation has long been recognized as a means to gain enlightenment, self-realization, and balance.

By exploring the different forms of meditation practice, you can discover what works best for you to enhance your overall well-being.

These practices share a common aim. You anchor your attention in the present moment and meet whatever arises without judgment. They're a good place to start if you're new to meditation.

Particularly accessible to beginners, mindfulness meditation invites you to immerse yourself in the present moment. It teaches you to observe your thoughts, feelings, and physical sensations without judgment. By focusing on the breath or the sensations in the body, rather than worries about the past or future, you learn to anchor yourself in the now. This can lead to reduced stress and improved well-being.

Read more: How to practice mindfulness meditation: 8 tips to get started

A body scan moves your attention slowly through the body, one area at a time, noticing sensation without trying to change it. It's a core mindfulness technique and a gentle way into the practice, since you always have your body to come back to. Many people use it to unwind before sleep.

Read more: Body scan meditation: a step-by-step guide to practice

Here the breath is your anchor. You rest your attention on each inhale and exhale, without trying to control the rhythm, and return to it whenever your mind wanders. Because your breath is always with you, this is one of the most portable practices you can carry into a stressful moment.

Read more: How to practice breath meditation to relieve stress

Mindful eating brings full attention to a meal through the senses: the color, smell, texture, and taste of your food, and the signals of hunger and fullness. It can support digestion and help you notice emotional eating patterns with more kindness.

Read more: How mindful eating can boost your mental and physical health

Focus is the whole point here. You rest your attention on one thing, a flame, a sound, or a word, and return to it each time the mind strays. Over time this can sharpen your attention and prepare you for deeper states.

Concentration meditation is a practice of focused attention, where the mind is directed toward a single point of reference, such as a candle flame, a sound, or the breath. This can help develop focus and attentiveness in everyday life and prepare the mind for deeper states of meditation. This practice can be especially helpful for students, professionals, or anyone looking to improve their cognitive abilities and productivity.

Read more: Concentration meditation: what is it and how to practice?

Mantra meditation involves the silent repetition of a word or phrase, known as a mantra, to focus the mind and bring deeper states of meditation. This practice can be found in many spiritual traditions worldwide and serves as a powerful tool for calming the mind, reducing stress, and enhancing spiritual growth and a sense of peace and clarity. If you find yourself easily distracted or struggling with a busy mind, mantra meditation can help you maintain focus.

Read more: Mantra meditation: what it is, benefits, and how to practice

Om meditation is a form of mantra practice built around chanting or silently repeating the sound "Om." The vibration of the sound gives the mind something to settle on, and many practitioners find the repetition grounding.

Read more: What is Om meditation? Plus, a step-by-step guide to practice

Also called trataka, candle meditation uses the soft glow of a flame as your focal point. You rest your gaze on the candle, placed at eye level a short distance away, and let it hold your attention. It's a visual anchor for anyone who finds eyes-closed practice restless.

Read more: Candle meditation: a step-by-step guide to practice at home

Visualization meditation guides you to picture a scene, place, or outcome in vivid detail, such as somewhere you feel calm and safe. By giving the mind a rich image to rest on, it can lower stress and support a sense of ease. Unlike open mindfulness, it actively directs the imagination.

Read more: Visualization meditation: 8 exercises to add to your practice

Warmth is the thread running through this family, directed toward yourself, the people you love, and even those you struggle with. These practices can enhance empathy and strengthen your relationships.

Compassion meditation aims to cultivate feelings of love, kindness, and compassion toward yourself and others. It involves silently directing sentiments of goodwill toward loved ones, acquaintances, and even people we might have a difficult relationship with. It encourages us to see beyond our differences, nurturing a sense of connectedness. A traditional example is Tonglen, a Tibetan practice that pairs the breath with compassion for suffering.

Read more: Compassion meditation: what it is, benefits, & how to practice

Loving-kindness, or Metta meditation, is a practice of cultivating an attitude of unconditional love and kindness towards all beings. It starts with developing a loving acceptance of yourself and gradually extends outward to include friends, family, strangers, and even those with whom we may have conflicts. This practice can help break down barriers of anger and division, promoting peace and connection. Though loving-kindness meditation and compassion meditation are closely related and often practiced together, loving-kindness meditation involves sending wishes of happiness, well-being, and peace to yourself and others, whereas compassion meditation specifically focuses on suffering, understanding it, feeling it, and wishing for it to be alleviated.

Read more: Loving-kindness meditation: what it is, how to practice and why

Gratitude meditation guides your attention toward what you appreciate, often by calling to mind a few people or moments you're thankful for. Practiced in the morning or evening, it can lower anxiety and support better sleep, and it pairs naturally with compassion practice.

Read more: How gratitude meditation can help reduce anxiety

Sitting still doesn't suit everyone. If stillness makes you restless, these practices let you meditate while the body is in motion, with your attention on each movement.

Moving meditation brings together deliberate movement and a focused mind. You move with purpose, noticing each step, stretch, or breath as you go. Yoga, tai chi, mindful stretching, and mindful dancing all fall under this heading, and it can be done almost anywhere.

Read more: The benefits of moving meditation and how to start practicing

Walking meditation means walking slowly and deliberately, with your attention fully on the act of walking: each step, the movement of your legs, the ground beneath your feet, and your breath. It turns an everyday activity into a practice you can take outdoors.

Read more: Walking meditation: how to practice and its benefits

Mindful movement is the broad practice of bringing present-moment awareness to how your body moves, whether through stretching, yoga, or simple everyday motion. It's mindfulness carried into the body rather than held in stillness.

Read more: What is mindful movement? Plus 9 ways to get started

Qigong is a traditional Chinese practice that combines movement, breathwork, and meditation to support balance and focus. In its active form you meditate while flowing through slow, deliberate movements, which makes it a bridge between exercise and stillness.

Read more: What is qigong? Benefits, types, and how to practice

Yoga nidra, sometimes called yogic sleep, is a guided rest practice done lying down. A teacher or recording leads you through stages of body scan, breath, and visualization into a state between waking and sleep. No yoga experience is needed, and it's often used for deep rest at bedtime.

Read more: What is yoga nidra? Plus, why it may be your new BFF at bedtime

A few practices are rooted in a specific lineage and tend to go deeper, often taught within a tradition or by a teacher. Several come from Buddhism, while others, like Transcendental Meditation and kundalini, come from Hindu and Vedic traditions.

Buddhist meditation is the broad tradition behind many of the best-known techniques, including Zen and Vipassana below, along with loving-kindness. If you want a feel for the tradition as a whole before exploring the specific practices, start here.

Read more: Buddhist meditation: types, techniques & how to practice

Zen meditation, or Zazen, is a disciplined form of meditation central to Zen Buddhism. You sit in a specific posture, focusing on the breath while observing thoughts and sensations as they arise and pass without attachment. Zazen emphasizes the importance of moment-to-moment awareness for a profound sense of peace and insight.

Read more: Zen meditation: how (and why) to start a Zen practice

Vipassana, which means "to see things as they really are," is one of India's most ancient techniques of meditation. It focuses on the deep interconnection between mind and body. Through disciplined attention to physical sensations, you can develop insight into the nature of impermanence, suffering, and egolessness, to move toward enlightenment and liberation. Vipassana meditation is challenging, yet rewarding, offering profound insights into our own minds.

Read more: Vipassana meditation: what it is, benefits & how to practice

Transcendental Meditation comes from the Hindu and Vedic tradition and was brought to the West in the mid-20th century. It's a silent, mantra-based practice, typically done for about 20 minutes twice a day, and it's taught one-on-one by a certified teacher who gives you your mantra. It's described as effortless, letting the mantra gently carry the mind toward deep rest.

Read more: Transcendental Meditation versus mindfulness meditation

Kundalini meditation is a spiritual practice that aims to awaken what the tradition describes as an inner energy at the base of the spine, using breathwork, posture, and chanting. Practitioners describe effects in spiritual and energetic terms, which are rooted in belief rather than established science. Because the breathwork can be intense, check with your doctor first if you have respiratory, heart, or blood pressure concerns.

Read more: What is Kundalini meditation? Plus, how to try it yourself

Chakra meditation focuses attention on the body's energy centers, or chakras, often by visualizing each as a spinning wheel, chanting a sound for each one, or breathing into each area. As with kundalini, the idea of chakras comes from spiritual tradition and is described as belief rather than scientific fact.

Read more: Chakra meditation: what it is, benefits & how to practice

This family works from the body inward. The practices help your nervous system slow down and your muscles release tension, which makes them a good entry point when stress shows up physically.

Relaxation meditation is an umbrella term for any practice that helps the body release tension and the mind wind down. It often draws on breathing, body awareness, or guided sessions, and it's a gentle place to begin if deeper practices feel like a lot right now.

A sound bath, where you lie back and listen to instruments, is one example.

Read more: 5 relaxation meditations that actually work

Progressive muscle relaxation guides you to tense and then release each muscle group in turn, usually working from the feet up to the face. By targeting the physical side of stress, it can ease tension that anxiety stores in the body, and it pairs well with a body scan to finish.

Read more: What is progressive muscle relaxation (and how to practice it)

The six families above group the 25 types by where your attention goes. This table puts them side by side, so you can compare meditation types and see where to start.

There’s no one-size-fits-all approach to meditation, and your needs may change over time. The best meditation technique is the one that feels right for you now and fits into your life. These five steps can help you find it.

Reflect on your goals: Think about what you're hoping to achieve. Whether it's reducing stress, finding inner peace, or developing compassion, your goals can guide your choice of practice.

Experiment with different practices: Try out different meditation methods and resources, including the Calm app, online courses, and local meditation groups, for guided sessions in different styles.

Listen to your body and mind: Pay attention to how you feel during and after meditation. The right practice should leave you feeling calm and refreshed. If you feel frustrated or strained, try a different type.

Be patient and open-minded: Finding the meditation practice that works for you can take time. Approach each type with curiosity, allowing your experiences to guide your journey.

Seek guidance: If you're unsure where to start, seek advice from experienced practitioners or meditation teachers for support tailored to your personal journey.

Starting a meditation practice can bring greater mindfulness and improved emotional well-being. However, establishing a consistent practice can feel daunting. By bringing these tips into your approach, you can build a meditation practice that fits your life and becomes a source of strength and peace.

A consistent routine is important in establishing any new habit, including meditation. Identify a time of day when you can dedicate a few uninterrupted minutes to your practice. For many, morning meditation sets a positive tone for the day ahead. However, if evenings or another time fits your schedule better, make that your meditation moment. Find a quiet, comfortable spot where you can sit or lie down without distractions.

💙 Morning person? Try this Morning Meditation to set your tone for the rest of the day. Nighttime person? This Deep Rest meditation is for you.

Begin with just a few minutes each day. Even a short period of meditation can have significant benefits, and starting small makes the practice feel more manageable. As you become more comfortable with the process, you can gradually extend your meditation time.

💙 Begin your meditation journey with Jeff Warren in this Meditation for Beginners series.

Meditation is a personal experience, and there’s no single "right" way to do it. Many people form expectations based on others' descriptions, but each person's meditation journey is unique. You don’t need to empty your mind or feel bliss in every session. Notice your thoughts and feelings without judgment, and meet whatever comes up with kindness and curiosity.

💙 Explore how having Expectations can affect us and our lives with Tamara Levitt on the Daily Calm.

Physical discomfort can be a distraction during meditation. To help settle your body and prepare for stillness, consider some gentle stretching or mindful movement, such as yoga or tai chi, before you begin. This can help release tension in the body and allow you to sit more comfortably.

💙 Follow along with The Daily Move’s Releasing Stress session to help you unwind before settling down.

Guided meditations, available through apps, websites, or local classes, can provide verbal instructions and cues to lead you through the meditation process. They can introduce you to different types of meditation, help keep your focus, and offer insights into how to handle common challenges like wandering thoughts or restlessness.

💙 Try this guided meditation to help you with Reframing Difficult Thoughts whenever your thoughts start to overwhelm you.

It’s normal to encounter challenges as you begin your meditation practice. You might miss a day or find your mind wandering constantly. When this happens, treat yourself with kindness and compassion. Each time you notice your mind has drifted and come back to the present moment, you’re practicing.

💙 Practice this Loving Kindness meditation to guide you in cultivating feelings of kindness toward yourself and others.

Join a meditation group or find a community of practitioners that can provide support and a sense of connection. Sharing experiences with others on a similar path can be incredibly encouraging.

There’s no single agreed list, but these three approaches are often used to sort the many types of meditation:

Concentration meditation: You rest your attention on a single point of reference, such as the breath, a mantra, a sound, or a candle flame. When your mind wanders, you return to it.

Mindfulness meditation: You widen your awareness to include thoughts, sensations, and your surroundings as they arise. It is often taught as part of Mindfulness-Based Stress Reduction (MBSR).

Loving-kindness meditation (Metta): You build goodwill and warmth toward others, starting with yourself.

The idea of levels in meditation can vary depending on the tradition or practice. A way to think of meditation depth might include the different types and states.

Physical relaxation: The initial stage where the body relaxes deeply

Concentration: The mind begins to focus, reducing distractions.

Mindful awareness: A state of heightened awareness of the present moment

Deepening peace: Inner silence becomes more profound.

Insight: Understanding about yourself and reality may arise

Blissful states: Experiences of joy or profound contentment

Transcendence: A sense of unity or oneness with all, often considered the deepest level of meditation

The power of a meditation practice can vary from person to person, depending on individual needs, preferences, and goals. Some may find mindfulness meditation helpful for managing stress and enhancing self-awareness, while others prefer the structured approach of Transcendental Meditation or the heart-opening practices of loving-kindness meditation.

Ultimately, the most ‘powerful’ type is the one you’ll keep practicing. Try a few to see which fits.

Napoli, Conte era il vero collante del gruppo: ora volano gli stracci

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Repubblica scrive a proposito del Napoli: “Sono volati gli stracci e ora è di dominio pubblico il vuoto di potere che dilania il Napoli, privato 5 mesi fa del suo unico collante dopo le dimissioni di Antonio Conte. Comandava lui e nessuno riesce a rilevarne in campo e fuori la leadership, tant’è che insieme alla squadra ha perso la bussola pura la società, a dispetto delle effimere rassicurazioni ricevute in estate da De Laurentiis dai suoi collaboratori e dai leader della vecchia guardia. Macchè ciclo agli sgoccioli, avevano infatti garantito all’unisono al presidente, convincendolo che bastasse un gestore come Allegri per restare lassù. Invece la ridistribuzione delle responsabilità è fallita”.

I Am Naples Testata Giornalistica - aut. Tribunale di Napoli n. 33 del 30/03/2011 Editore: Francesco Cortese - Andrea Bozzo Direttore responsabile: Ciro Troise © 2021 IamNaples Salvo accordi scritti, la collaborazione con questo blog è da considerarsi del tutto gratuita e non retribuita. Yesterday should not have been a surprise. The market has been overheated for past two years. The only real question is whether it will continue or rebound, and if we end up in a bear market (likely), how long it will last.

If we get a few more days like yesterday, I would consider putting some dry powder into the market. That is not an approved BH thing. Don't worry, it is less than 1% and I know, meaningless, so really just a psychological move.

Personal Investments • Re: Sell EE bonds and buy I-Bonds

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

I bought some EE bonds in 2021. Since it has been over 5 years, I want to sell it and buy I-bonds instead.
I am not familiar with what happens to the money when I sell the EE-bonds. I am hoping someone can help me out.

1. Does the cash after the sale of EE-bonds get deposited to my bank account or does it get deposited in an account within TreasuryDirect ?
2. Before filing taxes next year, will I get a 1099 form showing the interest from the sale of EE -bonds from TreasuryDirect ?

Thanks in advance.
Were those .1% or so back then? Terrible rate looking back. Remember if you hold for 20 years they are guaranteed to double, which would get you 2.4%. If you sell now youre locking in the .1% youve gotten.

I havent dont the math, i feel like it still makes sense to move out of them into the ibonds, but you should make sure.
